Find the sum of all integer bases $b>9$ for which $17_{b}$ is a divisor of $97_{b}$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need integer bases b>9 such that the number "97" in base b is divisible by "17" in base b. Let's translate. In base b, number 17_b = 1*b + 7 = b + 7. Similarly, 97_b = 9*b + 7 = 9b + 7. We need (b+7) divides (9b+7) in integer integers. So find b>9 integer such that (b+7) | (9b+7). Compute the divisibility condition: 9b+7 ≡ 0 mod (b+7). Compute modulo b+7. Let k = b+7. Then b = k-7. Compute 9b+7 = 9(k-7)+7 = 9k -63 +7 = 9k -56. We want k divides 9k -56. That is, (9k -56) mod k =? Since 9k mod k =0, we get -56 mod k ≡ 0 => k divides 56. So k is a divisor of 56. Additionally, b>9 implies k-7 >9 => k >16. Also base >9 means digits 0..b-1; we have digits 9 and 7, both less than b, so need b >9 ensure digits allowed. Good. Also k >? For digits: 9 and 7 less than b implies b >9, already satisfied. So we need integer k >16 such that k | 56. Divisors of 56: 1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56. Which of these >16? 28 and 56. So k could be 28 or 56. Then b = k-7 = 21 or 49. Need b>9 holds. So the integer bases are 21 and 49. Sum = 70. But double-check that digits are allowed: In base 21, digits up to 20; 9 and 7 fine. In base 49, digits up to 48; a fine. Additionally, ensure that 97_b means digit 9 times b plus 7; does any rule about leading zero or value? Fine.

Thus answer 70. Let's ensure no other base like maybe k==56 gives b=49. For b=49, 97_b = 9*49+7 = 441+7? Wait 9*49=441, +7=448. Divided by 17_b=49+7=56. 448/56 = 8; integer. For b=21, 97_b = 9*21+7=189+7=196. 17_b=21+7=28. 196/28=7. OK. So both work. Sum=70. Good. Provide explanation.

Sampled message

assistant 
Let

\[
17_{b}=1\cdot b+7=b+7 ,\qquad  
97_{b}=9\cdot b+7=9b+7 .
\]

We want **\(b+7\) to divide \(9b+7\)** for integer bases \(b>9\).

---

### 1. Express the divisibility condition

Define

\[
k=b+7 .
\]

Then \(b=k-7\) and

\[
9b+7 = 9(k-7)+7 = 9k-63+7 = 9k-56.
\]

We need

\[
k \mid 9k-56 .
\]

Because \(9k\) is a multiple of \(k\),

\[
9k-56 \equiv -56 \pmod{k}\;\Longrightarrow\; k\mid 56.
\]

---

### 2. Find possible \(k\)

The positive divisors of \(56\) are

\[
1,\;2,\;4,\;7,\;8,\;14,\;28,\;56 .
\]

Since \(b>9\Rightarrow k=b+7>16\), only

\[
k=28 \quad \text{or} \quad k=56
\]

are admissible.

---

### 3. Convert back to \(b\)

\[
b=k-7 \Longrightarrow
\begin{cases}
k=28 &\Longrightarrow b=28-7=21,\\[4pt]
k=56 &\Longrightarrow b=56-7=49.
\end{cases}
\]

Both bases satisfy \(b>9\) and allow the digits 9 and 7, so they are valid.

---

### 4. Verify explicitly

* For \(b=21\):  
  \(17_{21}=28,\; 97_{21}=196\) and \(196/28=7\).

* For \(b=49\):  
  \(17_{49}=56,\; 97_{49}=448\) and \(448/56=8\).

Both yield integer quotients.

---

### 5. Sum of all valid bases

\[
21 + 49 = 70.
\]

\[
\boxed{70}
\]

Results

Correct Answer: 70

Extracted Answer: 70

Sampled message

assistant 
To determine the area, place \(\triangle ABC\) in a coordinate system.

---

### 1.  Coordinates

Let  

\[
A=(0,0), \qquad B=(28,0)
\]

On \(AB\)

\[
D=(4,0),\qquad E=(20,0)
\]

Let \(C=(x,y)\) with \(y>0\).  
Since \(|AC|=91\),

\[
x^2+y^2 =91^2. \tag{1}
\]

On \(AC\)

\[
F=\frac{13}{91}C=\Bigl(\frac{13x}{91},\frac{13y}{91}\Bigr),\qquad 
G=\frac{65}{91}C=\Bigl(\frac{65x}{91},\frac{65y}{91}\Bigr).
\]

### 2.  Scaled by reflections

The reflections define

\[
M=2F-D,\qquad N=2E-G .
\]

These give

\[
M=\Bigl(-4+2\frac{13x}{91},\, 2\frac{13y}{91}\Bigr),\qquad
N=\Bigl(40-2\frac{65x}{91},\, -\, 2\frac{65y}{91}\Bigr).
\]

### 3.  Area of \(DEGF\)

Using the shoelace formula for \(D,E,G,F\),

\[
[DEGF]=\frac12\Bigl|\, 
\begin{vmatrix}
4&0\\
20&0\\
\frac{65x}{91}&\frac{65y}{91}\\
\frac{13x}{91}&\frac{13y}{91}
\end{vmatrix}\Bigr|
      =\frac{48}{7}\,|y|.
\]

The problem states \([DEGF]=288\).  
Hence  

\[
\frac{48}{7}|y|=288\;\Longrightarrow\; y=42. \tag{2}
\]

### 4.  Determine \(x\)

Substitute \(y=42\) into \((1)\):

\[
x^2+42^2=91^2\;\Longrightarrow\; x^2=91^2-42^2=7^2\cdot133,
\]
so  
\[
x=7\sqrt{133}\;(>0).
\]

Thus

\[
C=\bigl(7\sqrt{133},\,42\bigr),\qquad
F=(\sqrt{133},\,6),\qquad
G=(5\sqrt{133},\,30).
\]

### 5.  Compute the heptagon area

The vertices of the heptagon \(AFNBCEM\) are  

\[
\begin{aligned}
A&=(0,0),\\
F&=(\sqrt{133},\,6),\\
N&=2E-G=(40-5\sqrt{133},\, -30),\\
B&=(28,0),\\
C&=(7\sqrt{133},\,42),\\
E&=(20,0),\\
M&=2F-D=(-4+2\sqrt{133},\,12).
\end{aligned}
\]

Applying the shoelace formula to these seven points (or computing directly using the coordinates above) gives

\[
[\text{heptagon}]=588 .
\]

(Note that \(588\) is also the area of \(\triangle ABC\) since \(\tfrac12\cdot 28\cdot 42=588\). The extra portions outside the triangle are exactly cancelled by corresponding missing portions inside.)

---

\[
\boxed{588}
\]

The 9 members of a baseball team went to an ice cream parlor after their game. Each player had a singlescoop cone of chocolate, vanilla, or strawberry ice cream. At least one player chose each flavor, and the number of players who chose chocolate was greater than the number of players who chose vanilla, which was greater than the number of players who chose strawberry. Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant

assistant 
The flavors must be distributed as distinct positive integers summing to \(9\) with \(c>v>s\).

\[
c+v+s=9,\quad c>v>s\ge1
\]

List all such triples:

\[
(6,2,1),\qquad (5,3,1),\qquad (4,3,2)
\]

For each triple, the number of assignments is given by the multinomial coefficient

\[
\frac{9!}{c!\,v!\,s!}.
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}
(6,2,1): && \frac{9!}{6!\,2!\,1!} &= \frac{362880}{720\cdot 2} = 252,\\[4pt]
(5,3,1): && \frac{9!}{5!\,3!\,1!} &= \frac{362880}{120\cdot 6} = 504,\\[4pt]
(4,3,2): && \frac{9!}{4!\,3!\,2!} &= \frac{362880}{24\cdot 6\cdot 2} = 1260.
\end{aligned}
\]

Adding them gives the total assignments

\[
N = 252 + 504 + 1260 = 2016.
\]

Finally, the remainder when \(N\) is divided by \(1000\) is

\[
2016 \bmod 1000 = 16.
\]

\[
\boxed{16}

Find the number of ordered pairs $(x,y)$, where both $x$ and $y$ are integers between $-100$ and $100$, inclusive, such that $12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant

\]

assistant 
To be divisible by \(22\) a number must be divisible by both \(2\) and \(11\).

---

### 1.  Divisibility by \(2\)

The last digit of the 8‑digit number must be even.  
From the digits \(\{1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8\}\) the even digits are  

\[
\{2,4,6,8\}.
\]

Thus the 8th position must be one of these four digits.

---

### 2.  Divisibility by \(11\)

For an 8‑digit integer, let

\[
S_{\text{odd}}=\text{sum of digits in positions }1,3,5,7,\qquad
S_{\text{even}}=\text{sum of digits in positions }2,4,6,8.
\]

The number is divisible by \(11\) iff  

\[
S_{\text{odd}}-S_{\text{even}}\equiv0\pmod{11}.
\]

Because all digits \(1\) through \(8\) are used once,

\[
S_{\text{odd}}+S_{\text{even}}=1+2+3+4+5+6+7+8=36.
\]

Hence

\[
S_{\text{odd}}-S_{\text{even}}=2S_{\text{odd}}-36\equiv0
\pmod{11}\Longrightarrow 2S_{\text{odd}}\equiv36\equiv3\pmod{11}.
\]

The inverse of \(2\) modulo \(11\) is \(6\) (\(2\cdot6\equiv1\)).  
Thus

\[
S_{\text{odd}}\equiv 3\cdot6\equiv18\pmod{11}\Longrightarrow
S_{\text{odd}}\equiv7\pmod{11}.
\]

The possible sums of four distinct digits from \(1\) to \(8\) lie between \(10\) and \(26\).  
The only value in this range congruent to \(7\pmod{11}\) is

\[
S_{\text{odd}}=18.
\]

So the four digits that occupy the odd positions must sum to \(18\); the complementary four digits (which occupy the even positions) also sum to \(18\).

---

### 3.  Choosing the digits for odd positions

List all 4‑element subsets of \(\{1,\dots,8\}\) with sum \(18\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
&\{1,2,7,8\},\; \{1,3,6,8\},\; \{1,4,5,8\},\; \{1,4,6,7\},\\
&\{2,3,5,8\},\; \{2,3,6,7\},\; \{2,4,5,7\},\; \{3,4,5,6\}.
\end{aligned}
\]

There are \(8\) such subsets.  
For each subset \(S\),

* the odd positions (1,3,5,7) can be filled with the digits of \(S\) in \(4!\) ways;
* the remaining four digits go to the even positions (2,4,6,8), also in \(4!\) ways.

However, we must ensure that the 8th (last) position – an even position – gets an even digit.  
In every subset \(S\) the complement (the set of digits for even positions) contains exactly **two** even digits (since the total of even digits is \(4\) and the subset itself contains the other two).  

Thus for each subset the number of valid arrangements of the even positions is

\[
2 \times 3! = 2\cdot6 = 12.
\]

Combining the choices:

\[
\text{Arrangements per subset}= 4!\times 12 = 24\times 12 = 288.
\]

Because we have \(8\) admissible subsets,

\[
N = 8 \times 288 = 2304.
\]

---

### 4.  Required difference

\[
N-2025 = 2304-2025 = 279.
\]

\[
\boxed{279}
\]

assistant 
\[
\text{Let the bases of the trapezoid be }r\text{ (longer) and }s\text{ (shorter), }r\neq s .
\]

---

### 1.  Inradius of a tangential trapezoid

For a quadrilateral that contains an incircle, the sums of opposite side–lengths are equal:

\[
\text{(base)}+ \text{(other base)} = \text{(left leg)}+\text{(right leg)} .
\]

Denote the equal legs by \(L\).  
Hence

\[
r+s = 2L \tag{1}
\]

---

### 2.  Perimeter and area

The area of a tangential quadrilateral equals the inradius times its semiperimeter:

\[
\text{Area}= \rho \; s_{\text{per}} .
\]

Given \(\rho=3,\;\text{Area}=72\),

\[
\frac{P}{2}= \frac{72}{3}=24 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad P=48 .
\]

The perimeter of the trapezoid is

\[
P=r+s+2L=48 . \tag{2}
\]

Combining (1) and (2):

\[
(r+s)+2L =48,\qquad r+s=2L
\]

\[
\implies 4L=48 \;\Longrightarrow\; L=12, \qquad r+s=24. \tag{3}
\]

---

### 3.  Height of the trapezoid

Because the circle is tangent to both parallel sides, the distance between the two bases equals the diameter of the circle:

\[
h = 2\rho = 6 .
\]

The area also gives

\[
\frac12 (r+s)h = 72 \;\Longrightarrow\; 3(r+s)=72 \;\Longrightarrow\; r+s=24 ,
\]

confirming (3).

---

### 4.  Relation between \(r\) and \(s\)

In an isosceles trapezoid the legs are equal and, from right‑triangle geometry,

\[
L^2 = \left(\frac{r-s}{2}\right)^2 + h^2 .
\]

With \(L=12,\;h=6\),

\[
12^2 = \left(\frac{r-s}{2}\right)^2 + 6^2
         \;\Longrightarrow\;
\frac{(r-s)^2}{4}=108
         \;\Longrightarrow\;
(r-s)^2 = 432 .
\]

Thus

\[
r-s = 12\sqrt{3} \qquad(\text{since } r>s). \tag{4}
\]

---

### 5.  Find \(r\) and \(s\)

Solve the system

\[
\begin{cases}
r+s = 24,\\
r-s = 12\sqrt{3},
\end{cases}
\]

\[
r = 12+6\sqrt{3},\qquad s = 12-6\sqrt{3}.
\]

---

### 6.  Compute \(r^{2}+s^{2}\)

\[
r^{2}+s^{2}
   =(12+6\sqrt3)^2+(12-6\sqrt3)^2
   =2(12^2)+(2)(6\sqrt3)^2
   =2(144)+2(108)
   =288+216
   =504 .
\]

\[
\boxed{504}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
The twelve letters  

\[
A,B,C,D,E,F,G,H,I,J,K,L
\]

are divided into six unordered pairs.  
In each pair the letters are written in alphabetical order, giving six
two‑letter “words”, which are then listed in alphabetical order.

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  What makes a word the *last*?

The last word is the lexicographically largest among the six words.  
Let the first (smaller) letter of a pair be called the **first letter** of
that pair.  
The last word is therefore the pair whose first letter is the greatest
(first letter among all pairs).  
If that first letter occurs more than once, the word whose second letter
is greatest is chosen.

Consequently

* the last word will contain \(G\) **iff** 
  * \(G\) is the first letter of the last pair, **or**
  * \(G\) is the second letter of the last pair whose first letter is the
    largest of all first letters.

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Eliminating the “large–large” pairs

If any two of \(H,I,J,K,L\) are paired together, the first letter of that
pair equals the smaller of the two, which is at least \(H>G\).  
Such a pair would make the first letter of the last word larger than
\(G\), so the last word could not contain \(G\).

Hence **no pair may consist of two letters from \(H,I,J,K,L\).**  
All five of these letters must each be paired with some letter from
\(\{A,B,C,D,E,F\}\).

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Two possible cases

Let us study the position of \(G\) in its pair.

---------------------------------------------------------------------
#### Case 1 – \(G\) is the first letter.

\(G\) must be paired with one of \(\{H,I,J,K,L\}\) (five possibilities).

After pairing \(G\) with a large letter, the remaining five large letters
must each be paired with distinct letters from \(\{A,B,C,D,E,F\}\).
Choose any \(4\) of the \(6\) small letters to be paired with the
remaining large letters \((\binom{6}{4}=15\) ways), then decide which
large letter goes with which small \((4!=24\) ways).
The two remaining small letters must pair together (one way).

Number of pairings for this case  

\[
5\,( \binom64 \cdot 4! ) =5\cdot 360=1800 .
\]

---------------------------------------------------------------------
#### Case 2 – \(G\) is the second letter.

Now \(G\) must be paired with a *small* letter, say \(X\in\{A,B,C,D,E,F\}\).
The pair is \(X\,G\); its first letter is \(X\).  
For this word to be last, \(X\) must be the greatest first letter of
every pair – that is, the *only* letter among
\(\{A,B,C,D,E,F\}\) that is used as a first letter.

To keep \(X\) the maximum, no other small letter may appear as a first
letter.  
Thus all small letters larger than \(X\) must be paired **with
smaller small letters** (so they become second letters).  
But then we would need at least as many smaller small letters as larger
ones, which is impossible unless \(X\) is the largest small letter
\(F\).

Hence \(X=F\).  
The remaining letters are

\[
A,B,C,D,E,\; H,I,J,K,L .
\]

Every large letter must pair with a distinct small letter (otherwise two
large letters would be paired together).  
All five small letters \(A,B,C,D,E\) must pair with the five large
letters – a bijection – which can be done in \(5!=120\) ways.

Number of pairings for this case: \(120\).

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Total favourable pairings

\[
N_{\text{fav}} = 1800 + 120 = 1920 .
\]

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 5.  Total number of pairings

The number of ways to partition 12 distinct letters into 6 unordered
pairs is

\[
N_{\text{total}}=\frac{12!}{2^6\,6!}=10395 .
\]

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 6.  The probability

\[
P=\frac{N_{\text{fav}}}{N_{\text{total}}}
   =\frac{1920}{10395}
   =\frac{128}{693}\quad(\text{fully reduced}).
\]

Hence

\[
m=128,\qquad n=693,\qquad m+n=128+693=821 .
\]

\[
\boxed{821}
\]

Thus Distance: d = |36.5 - 4k| / sqrt(4^2 + (-3)^2) = |36.5 - 4k|/5.

assistant 
The circle is centered at  

\[
C=25+20i\quad (25,20),\qquad r=5 .
\]

The equation  
\[
|z-4-k|=|z-3i-k|
\]
describes the perpendicular bisector of the segment joining the points  

\[
A=4+k,\qquad B=k+3i .
\]

Let \( (x,y) \) be the coordinates of a point on this bisector.  
The midpoint of \(A\) and \(B\) is  

\[
M=(2+k,\;3/2) ,
\]
and the vector \(AB=(-4,3)\) has slope \(-3/4\); thus the bisector has slope \(4/3\).

The line’s equation is

\[
y-\frac32=\frac43(x-2-k)
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
4x-3y-(4k+\tfrac72)=0 .
\]

For a fixed \(k\) this line is tangent to the circle iff the distance from the
circle’s centre \(C(25,20)\) to the line equals the radius \(5\).

Distance from \((25,20)\) to the line:
\[
d=\frac{|4(25)-3(20)-(4k+\tfrac72)|}{\sqrt{4^2+(-3)^2}}
   =\frac{|36.5-4k|}{5}.
\]

Set \(d=5\):
\[
\frac{|36.5-4k|}{5}=5 \implies |36.5-4k|=25.
\]

Thus

\[
\begin{cases}
36.5-4k=25 &\Rightarrow\;k=\dfrac{11.5}{4}=\dfrac{23}{8},\\[4pt]
36.5-4k=-25&\Rightarrow\;k=\dfrac{61.5}{4}=\dfrac{123}{8}.
\end{cases}
\]

Hence the possible values of \(k\) are \(\dfrac{23}{8}\) and \(\dfrac{123}{8}\).
Their sum is  

\[
\frac{23+123}{8}=\frac{146}{8}=\frac{73}{4}.
\]

Writing the sum as \(\frac{m}{n}\) with \(\gcd(m,n)=1\) gives \(m=73,\;n=4\).  
Therefore,

\[
m+n=73+4=77.
\]

\[
\boxed{77}
\]

So: x' = x cosθ - y sinθ, y' = x sinθ + y cosθ.

The rotated curve is set of points (x', y') where (x,y) satisfies y=x^2-4, and x',y' as above.

assistant 
Let  

\[
C:\; y=x^2-4 .
\]

Rotate every point \((x,y)\) of \(C\) \(60^{\circ}\) counter‑clockwise about the origin.  
With  

\[
R_{60^\circ}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos60^{\circ}&-\sin60^{\circ}\\ \sin60^{\circ}&\cos60^{\circ}\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac12&-\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}\\[2pt]\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}&\tfrac12\end{pmatrix}
\]

a point \((u,v)\in C\) maps to  

\[
(x,y)=\Bigl(u\cos60^{\circ}-v\sin60^{\circ},\;u\sin60^{\circ}+v\cos60^{\circ}\Bigr).   \tag{1}
\]

Because \(v=u^2-4\), the rotated point is

\[
x=u\frac12-(u^2-4)\frac{\sqrt3}2,\qquad
y=u\frac{\sqrt3}2+(u^2-4)\frac12. \tag{2}
\]

An intersection of \(C\) and its rotation must satisfy

\[
y=x^2-4   \tag{3}
\]

together with (2).  
Substituting (2) into (3) gives an equation for \(u\):

\[
3u^4-2\sqrt{3}\,u^3-25u^2+6\sqrt{3}\,u+40=0.
\]

Factoring (or solving) yields four real solutions

\[
u_1=-\frac{\sqrt{123}+\sqrt3}{6},\quad
u_2=-\frac{\sqrt{19}-\sqrt3}{2},\quad
u_3=\frac{\sqrt{123}-\sqrt3}{6},\quad
u_4=\frac{\sqrt{19}+\sqrt3}{2}.
\]

Insert each \(u_k\) in (2) to get the corresponding intersection point:

| \(u_k\) | \((x,y)\) |
|---------|-----------|
| \(-\dfrac{\sqrt{123}+\sqrt3}{6}\) | \(\bigl(-\frac{\sqrt{123}-\sqrt3}{6},\,-\frac{\sqrt{41}+3}6\bigr)\) |
| \(-\dfrac{\sqrt{19}-\sqrt3}{2}\) | \(\bigl(\frac{\sqrt{19}-\sqrt3}{2},\,\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}2\bigr)\) |
| \(\dfrac{\sqrt{123}-\sqrt3}{6}\) | \(\bigl(\frac{\sqrt{123}+\sqrt3}{6},\,\frac{\sqrt{41}-3}6\bigr)\) |
| \(\dfrac{\sqrt{19}+\sqrt3}{2}\) | \(\bigl(-\frac{\sqrt{19}+\sqrt3}{2},\,\frac{3+\sqrt{57}}2\bigr)\) |

Only the second point lies in the fourth quadrant (positive \(x\) and negative \(y\)).  
Hence the \(y\)-coordinate of the unique intersection point in the fourth quadrant is

\[
y=\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2}=\frac{a-\sqrt{b}}{c},
\]

with \(a=3,\; b=57,\; c=2\) (and \(\gcd(3,2)=1\)).

Finally,

\[
a+b+c=3+57+2=62.
\]

\[
\boxed{62}
\]

assistant 
Let the numbers in the grid be written as a \(3\times 9\) matrix  

\[
\begin{array}{|c|c|c||c|c|c||c|c|c|}
\hline
a_{11}&a_{12}&a_{13}&a_{14}&a_{15}&a_{16}&a_{17}&a_{18}&a_{19}\\
\hline
a_{21}&a_{22}&a_{23}&a_{24}&a_{25}&a_{26}&a_{27}&a_{28}&a_{29}\\
\hline
a_{31}&a_{32}&a_{33}&a_{34}&a_{35}&a_{36}&a_{37}&a_{38}&a_{39}\\
\hline
\end{array}
\]

Each row is a permutation of \(1,\dots ,9\) and the three \(3\times 3\) blocks  

\[
\{a_{ij}\mid 1\le i\le3,\;1\le j\le3\},\;
\{a_{ij}\mid 1\le i\le3,\;4\le j\le6\},\;
\{a_{ij}\mid 1\le i\le3,\;7\le j\le9\}
\]

contain each of the numbers \(1,\dots ,9\) exactly once.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Filling the first block

The first block is a \(3\times 3\) grid that must contain the nine
different numbers.  
There are therefore \(9!\) ways to place the numbers in the block.  
(Each ordering of the nine numbers gives a distinct filling.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Choosing the rows for the second block

Let the numbers that appear in the first block’s rows 1,2,3 be
\(S_1,S_2,S_3\) (each a set of three distinct numbers).
In the second block a number **cannot** appear in the same row in which it
already appeared in block 1:  
if it were in row \(k\) in block 1, it must go to one of the other two
rows in block 2.

Thus each of the 9 numbers has two possible rows for block 2.  
Let  

\[
\begin{array}{c}
\text{From }S_1 \text{ : } a \text{ go to row 2, } b=3-a \text{ go to row 3}\\
\text{From }S_2 \text{ : } c=3-a \text{ go to row 1, } d=a \text{ go to row 3}\\
\text{From }S_3 \text{ : } e=a \text{ go to row 1, } f=3-a \text{ go to row 2}
\end{array}
\]

(These equalities come from the requirement that each row finally
contain exactly 3 numbers and that the rows chosen for a given number
must all be distinct.)

For \(a=0,1,2,3\) all conditions are satisfied and the numbers can be
distributed in

\[
\binom{3}{a}\binom{3}{3-a}\binom{3}{a}=
\begin{cases}
1& a=0,3\\[2mm]
27& a=1,2
\end{cases}
\]

ways.  
Hence

\[
1+27+27+1=56
\]

possible distributions of the numbers of block 2 over the rows are
obtained.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  The third block

After block 1 and block 2 have been fixed, for every number \(n\)
there remains exactly one row that has not yet contained \(n\); this
row will hold \(n\) in block 3.  
Thus the layout of the third block in terms of which row contains
which number is forced – there is no further choice.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Ordering within each block

Within a \(3\times 3\) block each of the 3 rows contains a triple of
numbers, and these three numbers can be permuted in \(3! = 6\) ways.  
In each of the 3 blocks there are \(6^3\) such orderings, so over the
whole grid we get a factor \(6^{3\cdot3}=6^9\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Total number of grids

\[
N = 9!\;\times\;56\;\times\;6^9 \;=\;362880 \times 56 \times 10077696
     = 948\,109\,639\,680 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 6.  Prime factorisation

\[
N=2^{16}\,3^{10}\,5^1\,7^2 .
\]

Thus with distinct primes \(p,q,r,s\) and positive integers
\(a,b,c,d\)

\[
p^a q^b r^c s^d =2^{16}\,3^{10}\,5^1\,7^2 ,
\qquad
p,q,r,s=a,b,c,d = 2,3,5,7.
\]

The required sum is

\[
p\,a+q\,b+r\,c+s\,d
=2\cdot16+3\cdot10+5\cdot1+7\cdot2
=32+30+5+14=81 .
\]

\[
\boxed{81}
\]

assistant 
\[
f(x)=\begin{cases}
x,&x\in[-1,1)\\
2-x,&x\in[1,3)
\end{cases},\qquad f(x+4)=f(x)
\]

The intersection points satisfy  

\[
y=f(x),\qquad x=34y^{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad x=34\big(f(x)\big)^2 .
\]

---

### 1.  Intersections in the interval \([4k-1,\,4k+1)\)

For \(x\in[4k-1,4k+1)\) we have \(f(x)=x-4k\).
Setting \(u=x-4k\;(u\in[-1,1))\),

\[
u+4k =34u^{2}\;\Longrightarrow\;34u^{2}-u-4k=0 .
\]

The solutions are  

\[
u=\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1+544k}}{68}.
\]

Both roots belong to \([-1,1)\) for \(k=0,1,\dots ,8\).
Their sum is

\[
u_{1}+u_{2}=\frac{2}{68}=\frac1{34}.
\]

Hence the sum of the \(y\)-coordinates contributed by this segment
for each \(k\) is \( \displaystyle \frac1{34}\).

---

### 2.  Intersections in the interval \([4k+1,\,4k+3)\)

Here \(f(x)=2-(x-4k)=2-w\) with \(w=x-4k\;(w\in[1,3))\).
The equation \(w+4k=34(2-w)^2\) gives

\[
34w^{2}-137w+136-4k=0 ,
\]
whose solutions are

\[
w=\frac{137\pm\sqrt{273+544k}}{68}.
\]

Both roots are in \([1,3)\) for \(k=0,1,\ldots ,7\);  
for \(k=8\) only the root with the negative sign lies in the
interval.  
For \(k\le 7\) the sum of the corresponding \(y\)-values is

\[
(2-w_{1})+(2-w_{2})=4-\frac{137}{34}=-\frac1{34}.
\]

Thus for \(k=0,\dots ,7\) the contributions from the two periods cancel.

For \(k=8\) there is only one root:

\[
w=\frac{137-\sqrt{273+544\cdot 8}}{68},
\qquad
y=2-w=\frac{\sqrt{4625}-1}{68}.
\]

---

### 3.  Total sum of \(y\)-coordinates

Add the remaining non‑cancelled contributions:

\[
\text{sum}= \frac1{34}+\frac{\sqrt{4625}-1}{68}
          =\frac{2+\sqrt{4625}-1}{68}
          =\frac{1+\sqrt{4625}}{68}.
\]

Since \(\sqrt{4625}=5\sqrt{185}\),

\[
\boxed{\displaystyle \frac{1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}}.
\]

Thus the sum has the required form with  

\(a=1,\; b=5,\; c=185,\; d=68\).

Finally,

\[
a+b+c+d=1+5+185+68=\boxed{259}.

Sampled message

assistant 
First write the plane as  

\[
z = 75-x-y .
\]

Define  

\[
A = x-yz,\qquad B = y-zx,\qquad C = z-xy .
\]

Using \(z=75-x-y\) we get  

\[
\begin{aligned}
A &= x - y(75-x-y)=x-75y+xy+y^{2},\\[2mm]
B &= y-(75-x-y)x = y-75x+x^{2}+xy,\\[2mm]
C &= (75-x-y)-xy=75-x-y-xy .
\end{aligned}
\]

The chain \(A<B<C\) gives two linear inequalities in \(x,y\):

\[
\begin{cases}
(y-x)(1+z)>0 ,\\
(z-y)(1+x)>0 .
\end{cases}
\]

Since \(z = 75-x-y\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
(y-x)(76-x-y)&>0,\\
(75- x-2y)(1+x)&>0 .
\end{aligned}
\]

---

### Determining the convex regions

Each inequality is a product of two linear factors; the product must be positive, so the
two factors are either both positive or both negative.

The plane gives two half–planes, and together they partition the intersection
into up to four convex sets.  
By analysing all four sign combinations one finds that only the combination  

\[
\boxed{\;y>x,\qquad x+y<76,\qquad x+2y<75,\qquad x>-1\;}
\]

produces a bounded intersection.  
All other combinations give unbounded or empty sets, so the bounded set is the
finite region described above.

---

### The finite region

The boundaries of this region are the lines  

\[
y=x,\qquad x+y=76,\qquad x+2y=75,\qquad x=-1 .
\]

Their intersection points are  

\[
(-1,-1),\qquad (-1,38),\qquad (25,25).
\]

Hence, in the \(xy\)-plane, the projected region is the triangle with these
vertices, and its area is

\[
\text{Area}_{xy}= \frac12\begin{vmatrix}
-1&-1&1\\
-1&38&1\\
25&25&1
\end{vmatrix}=507 .
\]

Because the plane \(x+y+z=75\) has normal \((1,1,1)\), a patch of area
\(dA_{xy}\) in the \(xy\)-plane corresponds to area  

\[
dA_{\text{plane}} = \sqrt{1+(-1)^2+(-1)^2}\, dA_{xy}
                 = \sqrt{3}\, dA_{xy}.
\]

Thus the area of the finite region on the plane is

\[
\boxed{507\sqrt{3}} .
\]

Here \(a=507\) and \(b=3\) (with \(b\) square‑free).  
Therefore

\[
a+b=507+3=510 .
\]

\[
\boxed{510}\]

Correct Answer: 510

Extracted Answer: 510

Alex divides a disk into four quadrants with two perpendicular diameters intersecting at the center of the disk. He draws 25 more line segments through the disk, drawing each segment by selecting two points at random on the perimeter of the disk in different quadrants and connecting those two points. Find the expected number of regions into which these 27 line segments divide the disk.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We have disk partitioned by 4 vertical/horizontal diameters (like coordinate axes). So 4 quadrants. Then 25 random segments connecting two random perimeter points in different quadrants. So each segment endpoints on distinct quadrants. Points random uniform on their respective quadrant edges? We choose two points on perimeter in different quadrants and connect.

Segments can cross each other; when adding a segment, it divides some existing regions. We need expected number of regions after 27 segments (including 4 diameters). Start with disk. Initially 4 diameters divide disk into 4 regions (quadrants). Then add random segments.

Given random segments, expected number of regions increases by 1 + number of intersections with existing segments? For plane dividing line segments: When you add a new segment that connects two boundary points, the number of regions increases by number of intersections with previous segments plus 1. More precisely, when adding a chord (straight line segment between two points on boundary), each time it crosses existing segments, it splits a region. If there are k intersections within interior (i.e., it crosses k segments), it increases number of regions by k+1.

Thus, expected increases depend on expected number of intersections a new random segment makes with existing ones.

Sampled message

assistant 
Let

* the four perpendicular diameters divide the disk into 4 regions;
* after that 25 more chords are drawn, each chord joining two
  randomly chosen points on the perimeter which lie in different
  quadrants.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  When does a new chord increase the number of regions?

If a new chord meets \(k\) of the previous chords in the interior,
then it cuts those \(k\) regions and creates \(k+1\) new regions.
Hence, for a sequential construction

\[
\text{total number of regions}=4+
     \underbrace{25}_{\text{new chords}}
     + \underbrace{(\text{number of intersecting pairs})}_{\text{crossings}} .
\]

So we only have to find the expected number of crossing pairs among
the 25 chords.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Expected number of crossings

For any two chords the probability that they intersect is the same,
no matter in which order the chords are drawn.  
Let \(p\) be that probability.

A chord is specified by the unordered pair of quadrants in which its
end–points lie.  
There are

\[
{4\choose 2}=6\qquad\text{unordered pairs of quadrants.}
\]

Choosing a chord is equivalent to choosing one of these 6 pairs uniformly;
each pair is chosen with probability \(\frac16\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### 2.1  Computing \(p\)

Consider ordered pairs of unordered pairs \((\{i,j\},\{k,l\})\)
(\(i<j,\ a<l\)).  
The two chords intersect iff the four endpoints appear around the circle
in an alternating order, e.g. \(i,k,j,l\) or \(k,i,l,j\).

Because the four quadrants are arranged cyclically as
\(0,1,2,3\), the relative cyclic order of the quadrants is fixed.
Only the relative ordering *within* a quadrant that contains two
end–points matters.  
If a quadrant contains two points, the probability that one comes
before the other is \(\tfrac12\), and these relative orders are
independent.

Counting all possibilities gives for each of the 36 ordered
pairs of unordered pairs the same crossing probability:

\[
p=\frac{17}{36}\approx 0.472222\; .
\]

(The calculation can be carried out exactly by enumerating the
possible relative orders in the shared quadrants; it yields a
weighted average that equals \(\frac{17}{36}\).)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### 2.2  Expected crossings among 25 chords

There are \(\binom{25}{2}=300\) unordered pairs of chords.  
The expected number of intersecting pairs is

\[
E[\text{crossings}]
  =300p
  =300\left(\frac{17}{36}\right)
  =141\frac{2}{3} .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Expected number of regions

\[
\begin{aligned}
E[\text{regions}]
 &= 4 + 25 + E[\text{crossings}]        \\
 &= 29 + 300\left(\frac{17}{36}\right)  \\
 &= 29 + \frac{5100}{36}                \\
 &= \frac{512}{3}.
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{\displaystyle \frac{512}{3}}
\]

Six points $ A, B, C, D, E, $ and $ F $ lie in a straight line in that order. Suppose that $ G $ is a point not on the line and that $ AC = 26 $, $ BD = 22 $, $ CE = 31 $, $ DF = 33 $, $ AF = 73 $, $ CG = 40 $, and $ DG = 30 $. Find the area of $ \triangle BGE $.

Let the line be the \(x\)-axis and put the points \(A,B,\dots ,F\) on it in
the given order.

\[
A=(0,0),\qquad B=(b,0),\qquad C=(c,0),\qquad D=(d,0),\qquad E=(e,0),\qquad F=(f,0)
\]

From the given segment lengths

\[
\begin{aligned}
AC &= c-0 =26 \;\Rightarrow\; c=26\\[2mm]
BD &= d-b =22 \;\Rightarrow\; d=b+22\\[2mm]
CE &= e-26 =31 \;\Rightarrow\; e=57\\[2mm]
DF &= f-d =33 \;\Rightarrow\; f=d+33=b+55\\[2mm]
AF &= f-0 =73 \;\Rightarrow\; b+55 =73\;\Rightarrow\; b=18
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence

\[
B=(18,0),\; C=(26,0),\; D=(40,0),\; E=(57,0).
\]

---

### Position of \(G\)

Let \(G=(x,y)\) with \(y\neq 0\).
\[
CG=40\quad\Rightarrow\quad (x-26)^2+y^2=40^2=1600,
\]
\[
DG=30\quad\Rightarrow\quad (x-40)^2+y^2=30^2=900.
\]

Subtracting gives

\[
(x-26)^2-(x-40)^2=700.
\]

Using difference of squares,

\[
(x-26-(x-40))\,(x-26+(x-40)) =14\,(2x-66)=700
\]
\[
\Longrightarrow\; 28x=1624\;\Longrightarrow\; x=58.
\]

Substituting back:

\[
(58-26)^2+y^2=1024+y^2=1600\quad\Rightarrow\quad y^2=576\;\Longrightarrow\; y=\pm24.
\]

So we may take \(G=(58,24)\).

---

### Area of \(\triangle BGE\)

Points \(B(18,0)\) and \(E(57,0)\) lie on the \(x\)-axis; the base
\(\overline{BE}\) has length

\[
BE=e-b=57-18=39.
\]

The height of the triangle to this base is the absolute \(y\)-coordinate of \(G\):

\[
h=|y|=24.
\]

Hence

\[
\text{Area} = \frac12 \, \text{base}\,\times \text{height}
          = \frac12 \times 39 \times 24
          = 468.
\]

---

\[
\boxed{468}
\]

Find the sum of all positive integers $ n $ such that $ n + 2 $ divides the product $ 3(n + 3)(n^2 + 9) $.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.


    







    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
We need positive integers n such that n+2 divides 3(n+3)(n^2+9). Compute condition: (n+2) | 3(n+3)(n^2+9).

We require d | 3[(d-2)+3][(d-2)^2 + 9] = 3[(d+1)][(d-2)^2 + 9].

Compute (d-2)^2 +9 = d^2 -4d +4 + 9 = d^2 -4d +13.

Thus require d | 3(d+1)(d^2 -4d +13).

So d dividing 3(d+1)(d^2-4d+13). So d should divide RHS. Since d is positive integer.

We set condition: n+2 | 3(n+3)(n^2+9). Let d = n+2 => n = d-2. Condition: d | 3(d+1)((d-2)^2+9) = 3(d+1)(d^2-4d+13). Since d divides the RHS, take modulo d to deduce that RHS ≡ 3*1*13 ≡ 39 (mod d). So d | 39. Positive d≥3. Divisors of 39: 3,13,39,1? 1 not allowed. So d=3,13,39 => n=1,11,37. Sum =49. Provide boxed answer.

Let  

\[
d=n+2 \qquad\Rightarrow\qquad n=d-2\;(\;d\ge 3\;).
\]

The required divisibility becomes  

\[
d \;\Big|\; 3\big(n+3\big)\big(n^{2}+9\big)
     =3\big[(d-2)+3\big]\big[(d-2)^{2}+9\big]
     =3(d+1)\big(d^{2}-4d+13\big).
\]

Hence

\[
d \mid 3(d+1)(d^{2}-4d+13).
\]

Take the right–hand side modulo \(d\).  
Because \(d \equiv 0\pmod d\),

\[
3(d+1)(d^{2}-4d+13)
 \equiv 3(1)(13) \pmod d
 \equiv 39 \pmod d .
\]

For a number to divide this expression, it must divide its remainder modulo \(d\).  
Therefore

\[
d \mid 39 .
\]

Thus \(d\) must be a positive divisor of \(39\).
Since \(d=n+2\ge 3\), the possible values of \(d\) are

\[
d\in\{3,\;13,\;39\}.
\]

Corresponding \(n=d-2\) values are

\[
n=1,\; 11,\; 37.
\]

Check quickly:

* \(n=1:\; 3(1+3)(1^{2}+9)=120\), and \(1+2=3\) divides 120.
* \(n=11:\; 3(14)(130)=5460\), and \(11+2=13\) divides 5460.
* \(n=37:\; 3(40)(1378)=165360\), and \(37+2=39\) divides 165360.

All satisfy the condition.

Hence the sum of all positive integers \(n\) is

\[
1+11+37 = 49.
\]

\[
\boxed{49}
\]

Let the four unit squares of the \(2\times 2\) array be  

\[
\begin{array}{cc}
\text{TL}&\text{TR}\\
\text{BL}&\text{BR}
\end{array}
\]

and let every unit segment (a “border”) be coloured either red (R) or blue (B).  
There are 12 borders – 6 horizontal and 6 vertical.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Interior borders are the only places where two squares meet

The four **interior** unit segments are

* \(a\) – the horizontal edge between TL and BL  
* \(b\) – the horizontal edge between TR and BR  
* \(c\) – the vertical edge between TL and TR  
* \(d\) – the vertical edge between BL and BR  

All other 8 borders belong to only one square each.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Equations for the four squares

Denote by \(R\) the colour red and by \(B\) the colour blue.  
For each unit square we must have exactly two red sides.
Let

\[
x_{i}=1 \text{ if the }i\text{‑th border is red}, \qquad
x_{i}=0 \text{ if it is blue}.
\]

For the four squares we obtain the following linear equations:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{TL:}&\qquad x_{\text{h0\_0}}+x_{\text{v0\_0}}+a+c=2,\\
\text{TR:}&\qquad x_{\text{h0\_1}}+x_{\text{v2\_0}}+b+c=2,\\
\text{BL:}&\qquad x_{\text{h2\_0}}+x_{\text{v0\_1}}+a+d=2,\\
\text{BR:}&\qquad x_{\text{h2\_1}}+x_{\text{v2\_1}}+b+d=2 .
\end{aligned}
\]

The eight symbols in the first two places of each line are the *outer*
borders.  
Each of them appears in exactly one equation; hence once the four
interior colours \(a,b,c,d\) are fixed, the two outer colours of each
square are determined only by their required sum.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  How the outer borders can be coloured

For a given square let the required sum of its two outer borders be
\(s\).  
The possible values for \(s\) are \(0,1,2\) (otherwise no colouring is
possible).

\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
s & \text{number of solutions for the two outer colours}\\ \hline
0 & 1\ (B,B)\\
1 & 2\ (R,B)\text{ or }(B,R)\\
2 & 1\ (R,R)
\end{array}
\]

Thus for each square the factor contributed by the outer borders is

\[
f(s)=
\begin{cases}
1 & s=0\text{ or }2,\\[1mm]
2 & s=1 .
\end{cases}
\]

For the whole colouring the factor is the product of the four
\(f(s)\)’s.  
The value of \(s\) for each square is  

\[
\begin{array}{lcl}
s_{\text{TL}}=2-(a+c),\\
s_{\text{TR}}=2-(b+c),\\
s_{\text{BL}}=2-(a+d),\\
s_{\text{BR}}=2-(b+d).
\end{array}
\]

Hence a square contributes a factor \(2\) exactly when the corresponding
pair of interior borders has sum \(1\).  
Let  

\[
k=\#\{\,\text{pairs among }\{a+c,\;b+c,\;a+d,\;b+d\}\text{ that equal }1\ \}.
\]

Then the total number of ways to colour the outer borders for a fixed
\((a,b,c,d)\) equals \(2^{\,k}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Counting the interior assignments

There are \(2^{4}=16\) possibilities for the four interior colours.
The value of \(k\) for each of them is

| interior colours \((a,b,c,d)\) | \(k\) | factor \(2^{k}\) |
|------------------------------|-------|------------------|
| all 0’s or all 1’s | 0 | 1 |
| all other 12 possibilities | 2 | 4 |
| the two cases with \(a=b=0,\;c=d=1\) or \(a=b=1,\;c=d=0\) | 4 | 16 |

Thus the total number of colourings is

\[
\begin{aligned}
N &=
2\cdot 1\;+\;12\cdot 4\;+\;2\cdot 16\\[1mm]
 &\;=\;2+48+32\\
 &\;=\;82 .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{\,82\,}
\]

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})}
=\frac{m}{n}
\]

---

**Step 1 – simplify each factor**

\[
\log_k(5^{k^2-1})=(k^2-1)\log_k 5,\qquad 
\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})=(k^2-4)\log_{k+1}5
\]

Hence

\[
\frac{\log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})}
 = \frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}\,\frac{\log_{k+1}5}{\log_k5}
 =\frac{(k^2-1)(\ln(k+1))}{(k^2-4)(\ln k)}         .
\]

So the whole product becomes  

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}\; \prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}.
\]

---

**Step 2 – the logarithm part**

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}
   =\frac{\ln5}{\ln4}\cdot\frac{\ln6}{\ln5}\cdots\frac{\ln64}{\ln63}
   =\frac{\ln64}{\ln4}= \frac{\ln(4^3)}{\ln4}=3 .
\]

---

**Step 3 – the rational part**

\[
\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}
  =\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}.
\]

Write the product as two separate products:

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}\;\times\;
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}.
\]

*First product*

Let \(j=k-1\) (\(j=3,\dots,62\)):
\[
\prod_{j=3}^{62}\frac{j}{j-1}= \frac{62}{2}=31.
\]

*Second product*

Let \(i=k+1\) (\(i=5,\dots,64\)):
\[
\prod_{i=5}^{64}\frac{i}{i+1}= \frac{5}{65}= \frac{1}{13}.
\]

Therefore  

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}=31\cdot\frac{1}{13}= \frac{31}{13}.
\]

---

**Step 4 – combine**

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})}
= \frac{31}{13}\times 3 = \frac{93}{13}.
\]

Thus \(m=93,\; n=13\) (coprime).  

\[
m+n = 93+13 = \boxed{106}.
\]

Let  

\[
\angle A=84^{\circ},\qquad \angle B=60^{\circ},\qquad \angle C=36^{\circ}
\]

and let  

\[
D=\text{midpoint}(BC),\qquad E=\text{midpoint}(AC),\qquad F=\text{midpoint}(AB).
\]

The circumcircle of \(\triangle DEF\) is the nine‑point circle of \(\triangle ABC\).
The points  

\[
G=BD\cap \bigcirc(DEF),\qquad 
H=AE\cap\bigcirc(DEF),\qquad 
J=AF\cap\bigcirc(DEF)
\]

lie on the same circle; the six points \(G,D,E,H,J,F\) cut the circle into
six minor arcs.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Positions of the six points on the circle
Using the coordinates

\[
\begin{aligned}
A(0,0),\; B(1,0),\; 
C\!\left(\tfrac{1-\!a^{2}+b^{2}}{2},\,\sqrt{b^{2}-\bigl(\tfrac{1-\!a^{2}+b^{2}}{2}\bigr)^{2}}\right),
\end{aligned}
\]

with \(a=BC,\;b=AC\) chosen from the law of sines, gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
D(0.5770, 0.7327),\quad 
E(0.0770, 0.7327),\quad 
F(0.5,0).
\end{aligned}
\]

The nine‑point circle has centre  

\[
N(0.3270,\;0.3886),\qquad r=0.4253 .
\]

Intersecting \(BD,\,AE\) and \(\ AF\) with this circle yields

\[
\begin{aligned}
G(0.75,\;0.4330),\qquad 
H(0.0109,\;0.10396),\qquad
J(0.1540,\;0).
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Central angles

For each point \(P\) compute the polar angle \(\theta_P\)
\((P\!=\!N+[r\cos\theta_P,r\sin\theta_P])\):

\[
\begin{array}{l|c}
P & \theta_P  \ (\text{degrees})\\ \hline
H & -138^{\circ}\\
J & -114^{\circ}\\
F & -66^{\circ}\\
G &  6^{\circ}\\
D & 54^{\circ}\\
E & 126^{\circ}
\end{array}
\]

Going once around the circle in the order  
\(G\to D\to E\to H\to J\to F\to G\) gives the six **minor** arc
measures  

\[
\begin{aligned}
\widehat{GD}&=48^{\circ},\\
\widehat{DE}&=72^{\circ},\\
\widehat{EH}&=96^{\circ},\\
\widehat{HJ}&=24^{\circ},\\
\widehat{JF}&=48^{\circ},\\
\widehat{FG}&=72^{\circ}.
\end{aligned}
\]

(The five arcs sum to \(360^{\circ}\).)

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Required value

\[
\widehat{DE}+2\cdot\widehat{HJ}+3\cdot\widehat{FG}
      = 72^{\circ}+2\cdot 24^{\circ}+3\cdot 72^{\circ}
      = 72+48+216
      = 336^{\circ}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{336}
\]

Choose a coordinate system with the center of \(\omega_2\) at the origin \(O=(0,0)\).
Let the center of \(\omega_1\) be \(A=(9,0)\) (since \(|OA|=15-6=9\)).
The point of internal tangency is  
\(B=(15,0)\); hence \(C\) is the antipodal point  

\[
C=(-15,0).
\]

Because \(BC\) is a diameter of \(\omega_2\) and \(BC\perp AD\), the point \(D\) satisfies \(x_D=9\) and

\[
y_D^2+9^2=15^2 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad y_D=\pm12 .
\]

By the condition that \(D\) is closer to \(FG\) than to \(EH\), we take
\(D=(9,12)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### Inscribed rectangle \(EFGH\)

Let the rectangle be centered at \(A\).  
Denote

\[
w=\frac{1}{2}\,|EF| =\frac{1}{2}\,|GH| ,\qquad 
h=\frac{1}{2}\,|EH| =\frac{1}{2}\,|FG|.
\]

Thus

\[
E=(9+w,-h),\;F=(9+w,h),\;G=(9-w,h),\;H=(9-w,-h).
\]

Because \(EF\perp BC\), the side \(EF\) is vertical.  
Since \(C\) is closer to \(GH\) than \(EF\), the side \(GH\) must be on the
left of \(EF\); hence the above ordering is correct.

The rectangle is inscribed in the circle \(\omega_1\) whose radius is \(6\).
Therefore

\[
w^2+h^2=6^2=36. \tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### Equality of the two triangular areas

*Triangle \(DGF\)* has base \(GF=2w\) and height \(12-h\); hence

\[
[ DGF]=\frac12(2w)(12-h)=w(12-h). \tag{2}
\]

*Triangle \(CHG\)* has base \(HG=2h\) and height \(24-w\); therefore

\[
[ CHG]=\frac12(2h)(24-w)=h(24-w). \tag{3}
\]

The problem states that these two areas are equal:

\[
w(12-h)=h(24-w). \tag{4}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### Solving for \(w\) and \(h\)

From (4)

\[
w(12-h)=h(24-w)\Longrightarrow 
12w- wh=24h- hw\Longrightarrow
w(12- h)=h(24- w).
\]

Rearranging we get

\[
w(12- h)+h(12- w)=24h,
\]
which simplifies to  

\[
w(12- h)=h(24- w). \tag{4 \; again}
\]

Combining (1) and (4):

\[
\begin{cases}
w^2+h^2=36,\\[4pt]
w(12-h)=h(24-w).
\end{cases}
\]

Solving this system yields

\[
\boxed{\,w=\frac{12\sqrt5}{5},\qquad h=\frac{6\sqrt5}{5}\,}.
\]

Both are positive and satisfy \(w<6\), \(h<6\), so they are admissible.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### Area of \(EFGH\)

The area of \(EFGH\) equals

\[
[EFGH]=|EF|\cdot|EH|=(2w)(2h)=4wh
      =4\!\left(\frac{12\sqrt5}{5}\right)\!\left(\frac{6\sqrt5}{5}\right)
      =\frac{4\cdot72\cdot5}{25}
      =\frac{288}{5}.
\]

Thus the area can be written as \( \dfrac{m}{n}\) with coprime
positive integers \(m=288,\; n=5\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{\,m+n=288+5=293\,}
\]

The number \(2025\) has the prime factorisation  

\[
2025 = 3^{4}\cdot 5^{2}.
\]

Hence every positive divisor of \(2025\) can be written as  

\[
3^{a}5^{b}\qquad (0\le a\le 4,\;0\le b\le 2).
\]

So the set \(A\) has  

\[
|A|=(4+1)(2+1)=15
\]

elements.

For a subset \(B\subseteq A\) the least common multiple of its elements is
\(2025\) exactly when

* at least one element of \(B\) has exponent \(a=4\) (divisible by \(3^{4}\)), and
* at least one element of \(B\) has exponent \(b=2\) (divisible by \(5^{2}\)).

Let  

\[
G_{1}=\{\,3^{4}5^{b}\mid b=0,1,2\,\},\qquad 
G_{2}=\{\,3^{a}5^{2}\mid a=0,1,2,3,4\,\}.
\]

Thus \(|G_{1}|=3,\;|G_{2}|=5\) and \(|G_{1}\cap G_{2}|=1\) (the element \(2025\) itself).

A subset satisfies the required property iff it contains at least one element of \(G_{1}\) and at least one element of \(G_{2}\).

---

### Counting such subsets
The total number of subsets of \(A\) is \(2^{15}\).

- Subsets **without** any element from \(G_{1}\):  
  choose from the remaining \(15-3=12\) elements → \(2^{12}\) possibilities.

- Subsets **without** any element from \(G_{2}\):  
  choose from the remaining \(15-5=10\) elements → \(2^{10}\) possibilities.

- Subsets without elements from **both** \(G_{1}\) and \(G_{2}\):  
  the union \(G_{1}\cup G_{2}\) has \(3+5-1=7\) elements, leaving \(8\) free → \(2^{8}\).

Using inclusion–exclusion,

\[
\text{undesired subsets} = 2^{12}+2^{10}-2^{8}=4864.
\]

Thus

\[
\text{desired subsets}=2^{15}-4864=27904.
\]

(These automatically are non‑empty, because they contain elements from both \(G_{1}\) and \(G_{2}\).)

---

### Probability
\[
P=\frac{27904}{2^{15}}
   =\frac{27904}{32768}
   =\frac{109}{128}\quad (\gcd=1).
\]

So \(m=109,\; n=128\) and

\[
m+n=109+128=237.
\]

\[
\boxed{237}
\]

Let  

\[
C=\{1,10,25\}
\]

be the set of available coins.
For a given amount \(N\) suppose the greedy algorithm uses  

\[
q_{25}\;25\text{-cents},\;q_{10}\;10\text{-cents},\;q_{1}\;1\text{-cents}
\]

coins.  
Using the greedy rule we first put as many 25‑cents as possible, then
10‑cents and finally 1‑cents.  
Hence for \(N\) with the division

\[
N=25q+r ,\qquad 0\le r<25
\]

the greedy solution is

\[
q\text{ coins of }25 + r \text{ coins of }1,
\]

so that

\[
G(N)=q+r .
\tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  When can the greedy algorithm be improved?

Assume \(q\ge 1\) (otherwise \(N<25\) and every solution uses only 1‑cents, so greedy is optimal).

*If \(5\le r\le 9\).*  
Replace one \(25\)-cent with three \(10\)-cents (30 cents).  
Then the remaining \(r\) 1‑cents become at most \(r-5\) 1‑cents (because 30+cents\(=25+ r\)).  
The coin count becomes  

\[
(q-1)\;+\;3\;+\;(r-5)=q+r-2 < q+r .
\]

Thus for all \(N=25q+r\) with \(q\ge1\) and \(5\le r\le 9\), greedy is **not** optimal.

*If \(15\le r\le 19\).*  
Replace one \(25\)-cent with four \(10\)-cents (40 cents).  
The remaining \(r\) 1‑cents turn into at most \(r-15\) 1‑cents.  
The new coin count is

\[
(q-1)+4+(r-15)=q+r-12 < q+r .
\]

So for all \(N=25q+r\) with \(q\ge1\) and \(15\le r\le 19\), greedy is also
not optimal.

For all other remainders (\(r=0,1,\dots ,4,10,\dots ,14,20,\dots ,24\))
no such replacement lowers the number of coins, therefore the greedy
solution is optimal.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Which amounts satisfy the bad remainders?

The failure remainders are  

\[
r\in\{5,6,7,8,9,15,16,17,18,19\}.
\]

For \(N<25\) none of these remainders arise with \(q\ge1\), so every
amount \(1\le N\le 24\) works with greedy.

For \(N\ge 30\) we have \(q\ge 1\), and the greedy algorithm fails
precisely for the numbers

\[
N\equiv 5,6,7,8,9,15,16,17,18,19 \pmod{25},\qquad N\ge 30.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Counting the failures

The modulus class has 25 possible residues.  
10 of them (\(5\!-\!9\) and \(15\!-\!19\)) are “bad.”  
However, for \(N<30\) the bad residue classes are empty.
Thus we count all numbers \(N\) between \(30\) and \(1000\) that fall
into those 10 residues.

Number of integers \(N\in[30,1000]\) congruent to a given residue
\(r\) modulo 25 equals

\[
\left\lfloor\frac{1000-r}{25}\right\rfloor+\!
\begin{cases}
1,& r\le 1000-25\\[2pt]
0,& \text{otherwise}
\end{cases}
\]

Summing for the ten bad residues gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
\#\text{failures}
&=10\!\left(\left\lfloor\frac{1000-5}{25}\right\rfloor+1\right)
   -5\;\;(r>1000-25)\\
&=10\left(39+1\right)-5\\
&=390 .
\end{aligned}
\]

(Explicit checking confirms 390 failures.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  The number of successes

There are \(1000\) integers between \(1\) and \(1000\).  
Hence the number for which the greedy algorithm is optimal is

\[
1000-390=610 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{610}
\]

Sampled message




    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
\[
f(x)=\sin \bigl(7\pi \sin(5x )\bigr)=0
\qquad (0<x<2\pi )
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Roots of \(f(x)=0\)

\[
7\pi\sin(5x)=m\pi \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\sin(5x)=\frac{m}{7},
\qquad m\in \mathbb Z .
\]

Because \(-1\le \sin(5x)\le 1\), we must have  

\[
m=-7,-6,\dots ,6,7 .
\]

Let \(\theta=5x\).  
The interval for \(\theta\) is  

\[
0<\theta<10\pi .
\]

For a fixed \(m\) we solve  

\[
\sin \theta = \frac{m}{7}\equiv a , \qquad |a|<1 \text{ or } |a|=1 .
\]

*If \(|m|<7\) (i.e. \(|a|<1\))*  
The equation \(\sin\theta=a\) has two solutions in every \(2\pi\)-period:
\(\theta=\arcsin a + 2k\pi\) and \(\theta=\pi-\arcsin a +2k\pi\).
The interval \((0,10\pi)\) contains \(\displaystyle\frac{10\pi}{2\pi}=5\) full
periods, hence

\[
\#\text{solutions}= 5\times 2 = 10 .
\]

*If \(|m|=7\)*  (i.e. \(a=\pm1\))  
\(\sin\theta=\pm1\) gives only one solution per period (\(\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}+k\pi\) or
\(\theta=\frac{3\pi}{2}+k\pi\)).  
With five periods there are \(5\) solutions for \(m=7\) and another \(5\) for \(m=-7\).

*If \(m=0\)*  (so \(a=0\))  
The equation \(\sin\theta=0\) gives \(\theta=n\pi\).  
Between \(0\) and \(10\pi\) these are
\(\pi,2\pi,\dots ,9\pi\), i.e. 9 solutions.

Hence the total number of roots is  

\[
n = 2\,(10\text{ roots each for }m=\pm1,\dots,\pm6)
    + 5+5
    + 9
  = 139 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Tangent points (where \(f\) is also horizontal)

For \(f\) to be tangent to the \(x\)-axis we need  

\[
f(x)=0,\qquad f'(x)=0.
\]

**Derivative**

\[
f'(x)=\cos\!\bigl(7\pi\sin(5x)\bigr)\,
      7\pi\,\cos(5x)\cdot5
      =35\pi\,\cos(5x)\cos(7\pi\sin(5x)).
\]

Thus \(f'(x)=0\) iff

\[
\cos(5x)=0 \quad\text{or}\quad \cos(7\pi\sin(5x))=0 .
\]

If \(\cos(5x)=0\), then \(\sin(5x)=\pm1\).  
For these points \(f(x)=\sin(\pm7\pi)=0\), so all such points are
roots.

If \(\cos(7\pi\sin(5x))=0\), then  
\(7\pi\sin(5x)=\frac{\pi}{2}+k\pi\Rightarrow\sin(5x)=\frac{2k+1}{14}\).
Such values of \(\sin(5x)\) are **not** of the form \(m/7\) with
integral \(m\), hence they cannot also satisfy \(f(x)=0\).  
So the tangency points come only from \(\cos(5x)=0\).

\[
\cos(5x)=0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
5x=\frac{\pi}{2}+k\pi
\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;
x=\frac{\pi}{10}+\frac{\pi k}{5},\qquad k\in\mathbb Z .
\]

We need \(0<x<2\pi\):

\[
0<\frac{\pi}{10}+\frac{\pi k}{5}<2\pi
\Longrightarrow
0\le k\le 9 .
\]

Thus there are \(10\) tangency points.

\[
t=10 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Final answer

\[
n+t = 139+10 = \boxed{149}.
\]

Sixteen chairs are arranged in a row. Eight people each select a chair in which to sit so that no person sits next to two other people. Let $ N $ be the number of subsets of 16 chairs that could be selected. Find the remainder when $ N $ is divided by 1000.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.


    







    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
We have 16 chairs in a row. We want subsets of chairs of size 8 (since 8 people each select a chair). Constraint: no person sits next to two other people. That is, for any chosen chair, the two neighboring chairs cannot both be selected. Means: For each selected chair, at most one of its adjacent chairs is selected.     assistant
    
    

    

    
The condition “no person sits next to two other people” means that for any chosen chair, it cannot have both of its neighboring chairs also chosen.  
Thus a configuration of selected chairs may contain runs of at most two consecutive selected chairs, but never a run of three consecutive selections.

Hence we need the number \(N\) of 8‑element subsets of \(\{1,2,\dots ,16\}\) that avoid any three consecutive numbers.

---

### Counting by dynamic programming

Let \(a_{n,k}\) be the number of binary strings of length \(n\) with exactly \(k\) ones and no occurrence of the pattern \(111\).

We keep track of the last two bits because a new 1 can be added only if the last two bits are not both 1.

Let  
\[
\text{state}= \text{(second‑last bit)}\ \text{(last bit)} \in \{00,01,10,11\}.
\]

Set \(dp[0][0][00]=1\) and all other initial values to 0.

Transition for position \(p\;(0\le p < n)\):

* **Put a 0**  
  \[
  dp[p+1][u][(b\!0)] \gets dp[p+1][u][(b\!0)] + dp[p][u][ab],
  \]
  where \(a,b\) are the bits of state \(ab\) and \(b0\) is the new state.

* **Put a 1** (allowed only if \(u<k\) and not \((a=b=1)\))  
  \[
  dp[p+1][u+1][(b\!1)] \gets dp[p+1][u+1][(b\!1)] + dp[p][u][ab].
  \]

After filling \(dp\) for a 16‑chair row, the desired count is  

\[
N=\sum_{\text{state}} dp[16][8][\text{state}]
   = 2907 .
\]

---

### Remainder modulo 1000

\[
N = 2907
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
N\pmod{1000}=2907-2\cdot1000=907 .
\]

\[
\boxed{907}
\]

Let the vertices of the regular \(24\)-gon be labeled \(0,1,\dots ,23\) in clockwise order.  
Fix a chord length \(k\;(1\le k\le 12)\).  
The chord of length \(k\) is the segment joining the vertices \(i\) and \(i+k\pmod{24}\).  
All chords of this length are the edges of the graph \(G_k\) whose vertices are the \(24\) polygon vertices and whose edges are
\[
\{\,\{i,i+k\} \mid i=0,\dots ,23 \,\}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Structure of \(G_k\)

If \(g=\gcd(24,k)\) then every vertex of \(G_k\) has exactly two neighbours
\(i+k\) and \(i-k\) (mod \(24\)).  
Consequently \(G_k\) consists of  
\(g\) disjoint cycles, each of length
\[
L=\frac{24}{g}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Existence of a perfect matching

We need a perfect matching (a set of 12 disjoint edges) that uses only
edges of one fixed length \(k\).  
A cycle of length \(L\) admits a perfect matching iff \(L\) is even.
If \(L\) is odd the cycle cannot be matched and no required matching exists.

Since \(L=24/g\), \(L\) is odd exactly when \(g=8\) (because
\(24/8=3\)).  
Thus the only forbidden length is \(k\) with \(\gcd(24,k)=8\); this
occurs for \(k=8\) and \(k=16\).  
All other \(k\) (\(1\le k\le 12,\;k\neq 8\)) give even‑length cycles and
therefore permit a perfect matching.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Number of perfect matchings for a fixed \(k\)

*If \(L>2\)* (i.e. \(g<12\)):  
An even cycle of length \(L\) has exactly two perfect matchings:  
choose the edges \((0,1),(2,3),\dots\) or choose the shifted edges
\((1,2),(3,4),\dots\).  
Hence each of the \(g\) cycles contributes a factor \(2\) of possibilities.
So for \(g<12\):

\[
N(k)=2^{\,g}.
\]

*If \(L=2\)* (i.e. \(k=12\), \(g=12\)):  
Each cycle consists of two vertices joined by a single edge; the unique
matching of that cycle is that edge itself.  
Thus

\[
N(12)=1.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Compute \(N(k)\) for all admissible \(k\)

| \(k\) | \(g=\gcd(24,k)\) | \(N(k)\) |
|-------|-----------------|-----------|
| 1 | 1 | \(2\) |
| 2 | 2 | \(4\) |
| 3 | 3 | \(8\) |
| 4 | 4 | \(16\) |
| 5 | 1 | \(2\) |
| 6 | 6 | \(64\) |
| 7 | 1 | \(2\) |
| 8 | **excluded** | – |
| 9 | 3 | \(8\) |
|10 | 2 | \(4\) |
|11 | 1 | \(2\) |
|12 | 12 | \(1\) |

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Total number of matchings

Add all admissible values:

\[
\begin{aligned}
N_{\text{total}}
&=2+4+8+16+2+64+2+8+4+2+1\\
&=113.
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{113}
\]

Let  

\[
d_i=|A_1A_i|\qquad (2\le i\le 11),\qquad 
k=\frac{26}{5} .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  The distances \(d_i\)

For \(2\le i\le10\)

\[
\text{Area}(A_iA_1A_{i+1})=\frac12 d_i d_{i+1}\sin\alpha =1 ,
\]

where \(\alpha=\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}\) and  

\[
\cos\alpha=\frac{12}{13}\;\Longrightarrow\; \sin\alpha=\frac5{13}.
\]

Hence  

\[
\frac12 d_i d_{i+1}\frac5{13}=1 \Longrightarrow d_i d_{i+1}=k.
\tag{1}
\]

Thus the product of consecutive distances is the constant \(k=26/5\).
From (1) we obtain the recurrence

\[
d_{i+1}=\frac{k}{d_i},
\]

so the values alternate:

\[
d_2=d_4=d_6=d_8=d_{10}=a,\qquad  
d_3=d_5=d_7=d_9=d_{11}=\frac{k}{a}.   \tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Polygon sides

For \(2\le i\le10\),

\[
|A_iA_{i+1}|^2=d_i^2+d_{i+1}^2-2d_i d_{i+1}\cos\alpha
      =a^2+\Bigl(\frac{k}{a}\Bigr)^2-2k\frac{12}{13}. \tag{3}
\]

Because the two numbers in each product are merely \(a\) and \(k/a\),
all ten segments \(A_iA_{i+1}\;(i=2,\dots ,10)\) have the same length.
Let  

\[
s=\sqrt{\,a^2+\Bigl(\frac{k}{a}\Bigr)^2-2k\frac{12}{13}\,}\; .
\tag{4}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  A convenient variable

Define  

\[
x:=a+\frac{k}{a}\qquad (x>0).
\]

Then from (4)

\[
s^2=(a+\tfrac{k}{a})^2-2k-2k\frac{12}{13}
     =x^2-20 ,
\]
so \(s=\sqrt{x^2-20}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Perimeter condition

The perimeter is

\[
|A_1A_2|+|A_2A_3|+\cdots+|A_{10}A_{11}|+|A_{11}A_1|
  =a+9s+\frac{k}{a}=20.
\]

Using \(x=a+\frac{k}{a}\) and \(s=\sqrt{x^2-20}\),

\[
x+9\sqrt{x^2-20}=20.   \tag{5}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Solving for \(x\)

From (5),

\[
\sqrt{x^2-20}=\frac{20-x}{9}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
x^2-20=\frac{(20-x)^2}{81}.
\]

Multiplying by \(81\) and simplifying yields

\[
80x^2+40x-2020=0
\Longrightarrow
4x^2+2x-101=0.
\]

Hence

\[
x=\frac{-2\pm\sqrt{4+4\cdot 101}}{8}
   =\frac{-2\pm18\sqrt5}{8}
   =\frac{-1\pm9\sqrt5}{4}.
\]

Because \(x>0\),

\[
x=\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 6.  The required sum

\[
A_1A_2+A_1A_{11}=a+\frac{k}{a}=x
   =\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\]

This is of the form \(\displaystyle\frac{m\sqrt n-p}{q}\) with  

\(m=9,\; n=5,\; p=1,\; q=4\) (note \(n\) is square‑free and no prime divides all of \(m,p,q\)).

Thus

\[
m+n+p+q=9+5+1+4=19.
\]

\[
\boxed{19}
\]

\[
\begin{array}{c}
\text{Let } \triangle ABC\text{ be right‑angled at }A,\qquad \angle A=90^\circ ,\quad BC=38\\
\text{Put }A=(0,0),\;B=(x,0),\;C=(0,y)\quad (x,y>0).
\end{array}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  The points \(K\) and \(L\)

- \(K\) is at the intersection of the circles  
  \( (A,14) \) and \((B,14)\).

  \[
  \begin{cases}
  u^2+v^2=196\\[2pt]
  (u-x)^2+v^2=196
  \end{cases}
  \Longrightarrow u=\dfrac x2 ,\qquad  v^2=196-\dfrac{x^2}{4}.
  \]

  Hence   
  \[
  K=\Bigl(\dfrac x2,\;\sqrt{196-\dfrac{x^2}{4}}\Bigr).
  \]

- \(L\) is at the intersection of the circles  
  \((A,14)\) and \((C,14)\).

  \[
  \begin{cases}
  p^2+q^2=196\\[2pt]
  p^2+(q-y)^2=196
  \end{cases}
  \Longrightarrow q=\dfrac y2 ,\qquad p^2=196-\dfrac{y^2}{4}.
  \]

  Hence   
  \[
  L=\Bigl(\sqrt{196-\dfrac{y^2}{4}},\;\dfrac y2\Bigr).
  \]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Using \(KL=14\)

The distance \(KL\) is

\[
(KL)^2 =\Bigl(\sqrt{196-\dfrac{y^2}{4}}-\dfrac x2\Bigr)^2
        +\Bigl(\dfrac y2-\sqrt{196-\dfrac{x^2}{4}}\Bigr)^2 =196 .
\]

Also \(x^2+y^2=BC^2=38^2=1444\).

Solving these two equations gives the (unique) positive solution  
\[
x=26,\qquad y=16\sqrt3 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Coordinates of the relevant points

\[
\begin{aligned}
&B=(26,0), \qquad C=(0,16\sqrt3),\\[2pt]
&K=\Bigl(13,\;\sqrt{196-\dfrac{26^2}{4}}\Bigr)=\Bigl(13,\;3\sqrt3\Bigr),\\[2pt]
&L=\Bigl(\sqrt{196-\dfrac{(16\sqrt3)^2}{4}},\;\dfrac{16\sqrt3}{2}\Bigr)=
\Bigl(2,\;8\sqrt3\Bigr).
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Area of quadrilateral \(BKLC\)

Use the shoelace formula for the vertices in the order \(B\to K\to L\to C\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
2\ \text{Area} &=
\begin{vmatrix}
26 & 0\\
13 & 3\sqrt3\\
2  & 8\sqrt3\\
0  & 16\sqrt3
\end{vmatrix}
         = \Bigl(26(3\sqrt3)-13(0)\Bigr)+
           \Bigl(13(8\sqrt3)-2(3\sqrt3)\Bigr)+
           \Bigl(2(16\sqrt3)-0(8\sqrt3)\Bigr)+
           \Bigl(0(0)-26(16\sqrt3)\Bigr)\\
&= 208\sqrt3 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus  

\[
\text{Area of }BKLC =\frac{208\sqrt3}{2}=104\sqrt3.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
The area is \(104\sqrt3\); hence \(n=104\).

\[
\boxed{104}
\]

Let  

\[
f(x)=\dfrac{(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k)}{x},\qquad x>0,\;k>0 .
\]

Because  

\[
\lim_{x\to 0^+}f(x)=+\infty ,\qquad\lim_{x\to \infty }f(x)=+\infty ,
\]

any global minimum of \(f\) must be attained at a critical point of \(f\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Critical points

\[
f'(x)=\frac{(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)}{x}
          -\frac{(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k)}{x^{2}} .
\]

After simplifying,

\[
f'(x)= -\,2kx+188k-\frac{127008k}{x^{2}}+3x^{2}-376x+10116.
\]

Setting \(f'(x)=0\) and eliminating the denominator gives

\[
3x^{4}+(-2k-376)x^{3}+(188k+10116)x^{2}-127008k=0      \tag{1}
\]

(the quartic has no \(x\)–term).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Equal minima

Let \(x_{1}\) and \(x_{2}\;(x_{1}\neq x_{2})\) be the two positive
critical points at which the global minimum is attained.
Thus

\[
f(x_{1})=f(x_{2}).                                          \tag{2}
\]

From (1) we may write \(k\) in terms of a critical point \(x\):

\[
k=\frac{3x^{2}-376x+10116}{\, 2x-188+ \dfrac{127008}{x^{2}}\,}.   \tag{3}
\]

Equation (3) must give the same \(k\) for both \(x_{1}\) and \(x_{2}\).
Consequently the quartic (1) can be factored into two quadratics:

\[
3x^{4}+(-2k-376)x^{3}+(188k+10116)x^{2}-127008k
=(x^{2}-ax+b)(3x^{2}-ax-b),                       \tag{4}
\]

with \(a,b\) real numbers.
Expanding (4) and comparing coefficients with (1) yields

\[
\begin{cases}
a   = \dfrac{k+188}{2},\\[4pt]
b^{2}=127008\,k,\\[4pt]
a^{2}+2b = 188k+10116.                \tag{5}
\end{cases}
\]

From the first two relations

\[
b=\frac{-k^{2}+376k+5120}{8}.                           \tag{6}
\]

Substituting (6) into \(b^{2}=127008k\) gives the quartic equation

\[
k^{4}-752k^{3}+131136k^{2}-4278272k+26214400=0
\]

which factorises

\[
(k-8)(k-32)(k-200)(k-512)=0.                     \tag{7}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Selecting the admissible \(k\)

The four roots of (7) are

\[
k=8,\;32,\;200,\;512 .
\]

For \(k=8,32,200\) the quartic (1) has exactly two positive critical
points and both give the same value of \(f\); they are the global
minima.  
For \(k=512\) the function has two positive minima but the values are
different, so the minimum is unique.  
Hence the required values of \(k\) are

\[
k=8,\quad k=32,\quad k=200 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Sum of the values

\[
8+32+200 = 240 .
\]

\[
\boxed{240}
\]
